Negative Worldview: Extreme beliefs such as 'The world is completely dangerous' or 'Life is meaningless'.\n  - Persistent Fear: A state of constant fear, anxiety, and tension.\n  - Persistent Horror: The shock and terror of the traumatic moment do not fade and remain.\n  - Persistent Anger: A state of chronic anger boiling inside or being expressed aggressively.\n  - Persistent Guilt: Unceasing feelings of guilt or remorse related to the trauma.\n  - Persistent Shame: Continuing to feel shame or humiliation due to the trauma.\n▸ arousal_and_reactivity\n  - Reduced Concentration: Unable to pay attention or maintain focus on specific activities like conversation, reading, or watching TV.\n  - Indecisiveness: Extreme difficulty making even minor daily choices (e.g., menu, clothes) and hesitating to make decisions.\n  - Subjective Memory Complaints: Complaining of memory decline, such as forgetting where things were placed, due to reduced focus.\n  - Clouded Thinking: Thinking feels foggy, dazed, or not clear.\n  - Impulsive Anger: Sudden explosions of anger for trivial reasons or no clear reason.\n  - Verbal Aggression: Attacking others with shouting, cursing, or blame without holding back.\n  - Physical Aggression: Physically attacking people or throwing/breaking objects.\n  - Chronic Irritability: A state where nerves are always sharp and sensitive, ready to explode at the slightest touch.\n  - Negative Self-Beliefs: Distorted beliefs such as 'I am fundamentally bad,' 'I am damaged,' or 'I am worthless'.\n  - Negative Beliefs about Others: Distorted beliefs such as 'No one can be trusted' or 'Others are dangerous'.\n  - Negative Worldview: Extreme beliefs such as 'The world is completely dangerous' or 'Life is meaningless'.\n  - Persistent Fear: A state of constant fear, anxiety, and tension.\n  - Persistent Horror: The shock and terror of the traumatic moment do not fade and remain.\n  - Persistent Anger: A state of chronic anger boiling inside or being expressed aggressively.\n  - Persistent Guilt: Unceasing feelings of guilt or remorse related to the trauma.\n  - Persistent Shame: Continuing to feel shame or humiliation due to the trauma.\n▸ arousal_and_reactivity\n  - Reduced Concentration: Unable to pay attention or maintain focus on specific activities like conversation, reading, or watching TV.\n  - Indecisiveness: Extreme difficulty making even minor daily choices (e.g., menu, clothes) and hesitating to make decisions.\n  - Subjective Memory Complaints: Complaining of memory decline, such as forgetting where things were placed, due to reduced focus.\n  - Clouded Thinking: Thinking feels foggy, dazed, or not clear.\n  - Impulsive Anger: Sudden explosions of anger for trivial reasons or no clear reason.\n  - Verbal Aggression: Attacking others with shouting, cursing, or blame without holding back.\n  - Physical Aggression: Physically attacking people or throwing/breaking objects.\n  - Chronic Irritability: A state where nerves are always sharp and sensitive, ready to explode at the slightest touch.\n  - Risky Behaviors: Reckless actions disregarding consequences, such as speeding, dangerous sports, or criminal acts.\n  - Substance Abuse: Excessive drinking or drug use to harm oneself.\n  - Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior: Engaging in unprotected or dangerous sexual activities repetitively.\n  - Self-Harm: Actions that intentionally harm or scar one's own body.\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - Duration: 3 months\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: Intrusive_Symptoms\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks): Dissociative reactions (e.g., flashbacks) in which the individual feels or acts as if the traumatic event were recurring.\n  - Psychological Distress at Cues: Intense or prolonged psychological distress at exposure to internal or external cues that symbolize or resemble an aspect of the traumatic event.\n  - Negative Worldview: Extreme beliefs such as 'The world is completely dangerous' or 'Life is meaningless'.\n  - Persistent Fear: A state of constant fear, anxiety, and tension.\n  - Persistent Horror: The shock and terror of the traumatic moment do not fade and remain.\n  - Persistent Anger: A state of chronic anger boiling inside or being expressed aggressively.\n  - Persistent Guilt: Unceasing feelings of guilt or remorse related to the trauma.\n  - Persistent Shame: Continuing to feel shame or humiliation due to the trauma.\n▸ arousal_and_reactivity\n  - I'm just so exhausted because it feels like I haven't had a single moment of peace.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the bad event, or are there any things that you used to enjoy but now avoid because of the memories or feelings they bring up?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely do. I completely avoid the neighborhood where it happened, even if it means taking a much longer route to get anywhere. I’ve also been staying away from social gatherings and my friends because I'm terrified that someone will bring it up.\n\nDoctor: Since your symptoms have been present for about three months now, can you tell me if they have been continuous, or have you noticed any periods where they seemed to lessen or disappear, even if briefly?\n\nPatient: Yes, they have been completely continuous and constant for the entire three months. I haven't had any real breaks or periods where they went away or even lessened. It just feels like an unrelenting weight every single day.\n\nDoctor: How have these symptoms and the avoidance behaviors been affecting your daily life, relationships, and overall well-being, and are you experiencing any other emotions like guilt, shame, or anxiety related to the event?\n\nPatient: Avoiding any reminders or thoughts of what happened has completely ruined my relationships and left me feeling incredibly isolated from everyone. On top of that, I'm living in constant, exhausting fear and carrying a heavy burden of guilt and shame over what happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any persistent feelings of sadness, hopelessness, or loss of interest in activities you once enjoyed, beyond the feelings of guilt and shame related to the event?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel a general sense of sadness or a loss of interest in things on their own. Instead, I just feel completely consumed by the constant guilt, shame, and fear directly related to the trauma. Those feelings are so overwhelming that they don't leave room for anything else."

The patient's description of being consumed by guilt, shame, and fear related to the trauma, without mentioning a general sense of sadness or loss of interest in activities that would be characteristic of Major Depressive Disorder, makes PTSD a more likely diagnosis. Therefore, Major Depressive Disorder seems less likely based on the information provided, leading to its removal from the candidate list.", "is_final": false}

Intrusive Memories: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.\n  - Distressing Dreams: Recurrent distressing dreams in which the content and/or affect of the dream are related to the traumatic event.\n  - Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks): Dissociative reactions (e.g., flashbacks) in which the individual feels or acts as if the traumatic event were recurring.\n  - Psychological Distress at Cues: Intense or prolonged psychological distress at exposure to internal or external cues that symbolize or resemble an aspect of the traumatic event.\n  - Physiological Reactions to Cues: Marked physiological reactions to internal or external cues that symbolize or resemble an aspect of the traumatic event.\n  - Internal Avoidance: Efforts to avoid distressing memories, thoughts, or feelings about or closely associated with the traumatic event.\n  - External Avoidance: Efforts to avoid external reminders (people, places, conversations, activities, objects, situations) that arouse distressing memories, thoughts, or feelings.\n▸ trauma_related_alterations\n  - Negative Self-Beliefs: Distorted beliefs such as 'I am fundamentally bad,' 'I am damaged,' or 'I am worthless'.\n  - Negative Beliefs about Others: Distorted beliefs such as 'No one can be trusted' or 'Others are dangerous'.\n  - Negative Worldview: Extreme beliefs such as 'The world is completely dangerous' or 'Life is meaningless'.\n  - Persistent Fear: A state of constant fear, anxiety, and tension.\n  - Persistent Horror: The shock and terror of the traumatic moment do not fade and remain.\n  - Persistent Anger: A state of chronic anger boiling inside or being expressed aggressively.\n  - Persistent Guilt: Unceasing feelings of guilt or remorse related to the trauma.\n  - Persistent Shame: Continuing to feel shame or humiliation due to the trauma.\n▸ arousal_and_reactivity\n  - Reduced Concentration: Unable to pay attention or maintain focus on specific activities like conversation, reading, or watching TV.\n  - Indecisiveness: Extreme difficulty making even minor daily choices (e.g., menu, clothes) and hesitating to make decisions.\n  - Subjective Memory Complaints: Complaining of memory decline, such as forgetting where things were placed, due to reduced focus.\n  - Clouded Thinking: Thinking feels foggy, dazed, or not clear.\n  - Impulsive Anger: Sudden explosions of anger for trivial reasons or no clear reason.\n  - Verbal Aggression: Attacking others with shouting, cursing, or blame without holding back.\n  - Physical Aggression: Physically attacking people or throwing/breaking objects.\n  - Chronic Irritability: A state where nerves are always sharp and sensitive, ready to explode at the slightest touch.\n  - Risky Behaviors: Reckless actions disregarding consequences, such as speeding, dangerous sports, or criminal acts.\n  - Substance Abuse: Excessive drinking or drug use to harm oneself.\n  - Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior: Engaging in unprotected or dangerous sexual activities repetitively.\n  - I'm just so exhausted because it feels like I haven't had a single moment of peace.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the bad event, or are there any things that you used to enjoy but now avoid because of the memories or feelings they bring up?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely do.     "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are an assistant analyst connecting the [Symptom Profile] with the doctor's question.\nDetermine which part of the profile (chief complaint, associated symptoms, social history, etc.)\nthe doctor's **last question** is related to.\n\n[Symptom Profile]\n▸ trauma_related_symptoms\n  - Intrusive Memories: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.\n  - Distressing Dreams: Recurrent distressing dreams in which the content and/or affect of the dream are related to the traumatic event.\n  - Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks): Dissociative reactions (e.g., flashbacks) in which the individual feels or acts as if the traumatic event were recurring.\n  - Psychological Distress at Cues: Intense or prolonged psychological distress at exposure to internal or external cues that symbolize or resemble an aspect of the traumatic event.\n  - Physiological Reactions to Cues: Marked physiological reactions to internal or external cues that symbolize or resemble an aspect of the traumatic event.\n  - Internal Avoidance: Efforts to avoid distressing memories, thoughts, or feelings about or closely associated with the traumatic event.\n  - External Avoidance: Efforts to avoid external reminders (people, places, conversations, activities, objects, situations) that arouse distressing memories, thoughts, or feelings.\n▸ trauma_related_alterations\n  - Negative Self-Beliefs: Distorted beliefs such as 'I am fundamentally bad,' 'I am damaged,' or 'I am worthless'.\n  - Negative Beliefs about Others: Distorted beliefs such as 'No one can be trusted' or 'Others are dangerous'.\n  - Negative Worldview: Extreme beliefs such as 'The world is completely dangerous' or 'Life is meaningless'.\n  - Persistent Fear: A state of constant fear, anxiety, and tension.\n  - Persistent Horror: The shock and terror of the traumatic moment do not fade and remain.\n  - Persistent Anger: A state of chronic anger boiling inside or being expressed aggressively.\n  - Persistent Guilt: Unceasing feelings of guilt or remorse related to the trauma.\n  - Persistent Shame: Continuing to feel shame or humiliation due to the trauma.\n▸ arousal_and_reactivity\n  - Reduced Concentration: Unable to pay attention or maintain focus on specific activities like conversation, reading, or watching TV.\n  - Indecisiveness: Extreme difficulty making even minor daily choices (e.g., menu, clothes) and hesitating to make decisions.\n  - Subjective Memory Complaints: Complaining of memory decline, such as forgetting where things were placed, due to reduced focus.\n  - Clouded Thinking: Thinking feels foggy, dazed, or not clear.\n  - Impulsive Anger: Sudden explosions of anger for trivial reasons or no clear reason.\n  - Verbal Aggression: Attacking others with shouting, cursing, or blame without holding back.\n  - Physical Aggression: Physically attacking people or throwing/breaking objects.\n  - Chronic Irritability: A state where nerves are always sharp and sensitive, ready to explode at the slightest touch.\n  - Risky Behaviors: Reckless actions disregarding consequences, such as speeding, dangerous sports, or criminal acts.\n  - Substance Abuse: Excessive drinking or drug use to harm oneself.\n  - Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior: Engaging in unprotected or dangerous sexual activities repetitively.\n  - Self-Harm: Actions that intentionally harm or scar one's own body.\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - Duration: 3 months\n\nRules:\n- If the patient can specifically answer based on the profile, set matched=true, list related\n  sections/items in matched_sections, and write a short English strategy in answer_strategy\n  on what tone and content the patient should reply with.\n- If the question is not in the profile or can only be inferred, set matched=false and instruct\n  in answer_strategy to respond briefly (e.g., \"I'm not sure\", \"I haven't thought about it\").\n- If patient_turn_index is 1 (patient's first reply), ONLY focus the strategy on the chief complaint\n  (do NOT reveal other symptoms).\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"matched\": true|false, \"matched_sections\": [\"...\"], \"answer_strategy\": \"...\"}\n"

Persistent Anger: A state of chronic anger boiling inside or being expressed aggressively.\n  - Persistent Guilt: Unceasing feelings of guilt or remorse related to the trauma.\n  - Persistent Shame: Continuing to feel shame or humiliation due to the trauma.\n▸ arousal_and_reactivity\n  - Reduced Concentration: Unable to pay attention or maintain focus on specific activities like conversation, reading, or watching TV.\n  - Indecisiveness: Extreme difficulty making even minor daily choices (e.g., menu, clothes) and hesitating to make decisions.\n  - Subjective Memory Complaints: Complaining of memory decline, such as forgetting where things were placed, due to reduced focus.\n  - Clouded Thinking: Thinking feels foggy, dazed, or not clear.\n  - Impulsive Anger: Sudden explosions of anger for trivial reasons or no clear reason.\n  - Verbal Aggression: Attacking others with shouting, cursing, or blame without holding back.\n  - Physical Aggression: Physically attacking people or throwing/breaking objects.\n  - Chronic Irritability: A state where nerves are always sharp and sensitive, ready to explode at the slightest touch.\n  - Risky Behaviors: Reckless actions disregarding consequences, such as speeding, dangerous sports, or criminal acts.\n  - Substance Abuse: Excessive drinking or drug use to harm oneself.\n  - Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior: Engaging in unprotected or dangerous sexual activities repetitively.\n  - Self-Harm: Actions that intentionally harm or scar one's own body.\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - Duration: 3 months\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: Intrusive_Symptoms\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(No match in profile or inference only)\nAnswer strategy: Deny experiencing any unusual perceptions, such as hearing voices, seeing things that aren't there, or holding unrealistic or illogical beliefs."
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm here because I've been struggling with really distressing, intrusive memories and awful nightmares about a bad event that happened a few months ago. It's starting to completely overwhelm me, and I just don't know how to make them stop.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about when these symptoms started and how long you've been experiencing them, specifically in relation to the bad event that happened a few months ago?\n\nPatient: They started almost immediately after the event, which happened about three months ago. It's been happening continuously every single day and night since then. I'm just so exhausted because it feels like I haven't had a single moment of peace.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the bad event, or are there any things that you used to enjoy but now avoid because of the memories or feelings they bring up?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely do. I completely avoid the neighborhood where it happened, even if it means taking a much longer route to get anywhere. I’ve also been staying away from social gatherings and my friends because I'm terrified that someone will bring it up.\n\nDoctor: Since your symptoms have been present for about three months now, can you tell me if they have been continuous, or have you noticed any periods where they seemed to lessen or disappear, even if briefly?\n\nPatient: Yes, they have been completely continuous and constant for the entire three months. I haven't had any real breaks or periods where they went away or even lessened. On top of that, I'm living in constant, exhausting fear and carrying a heavy burden of guilt and shame over what happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any persistent feelings of sadness, hopelessness, or loss of interest in activities you once enjoyed, beyond the feelings of guilt and shame related to the event?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel a general sense of sadness or a loss of interest in things on their own. Instead, I just feel completely consumed by the constant guilt, shame, and fear directly related to the trauma. Those feelings are so overwhelming that they don't leave room for anything else.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any difficulty sleeping, irritability, or difficulty concentrating, and do you find yourself constantly 'on guard' or easily startled, as if you're always waiting for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: I'm having a really hard time concentrating on daily tasks and conversations, and my nerves are so sharp that I feel constantly irritable. I wouldn't say I feel especially 'on guard' or easily startled, though. It's really just the lack of focus and this constant irritability that are making things so difficult.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any unusual perceptions, such as hearing voices or seeing things that others don't, or do you have any strong beliefs that seem unreal or illogical to others, especially in relation to the traumatic event?"

No, I haven't experienced anything like that at all. I don't hear voices or see things that aren't there, and I don't hold any beliefs that are unreal or illogical. My mind doesn't play tricks on me in that way.

However, the duration of symptoms is approximately three months, which is around the threshold for differentiating between Acute Stress Disorder (ASD) and PTSD. ASD typically lasts up to a month, but the criteria for PTSD include symptoms lasting more than a month. Given the continuous nature of the symptoms and their impact on daily life, PTSD seems more likely, but the timeframe and the possibility that symptoms could still evolve warrant considering ASD as well, although less likely due to the duration. The absence of symptoms like hearing voices, seeing things that aren't there, or holding unreal beliefs helps to rule out psychotic disorders from the list. Intrusive Memories: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.\n  - Distressing Dreams: Recurrent distressing dreams in which the content and/or affect of the dream are related to the traumatic event.\n  - Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks): Dissociative reactions (e.g., flashbacks) in which the individual feels or acts as if the traumatic event were recurring.\n  - Psychological Distress at Cues: Intense or prolonged psychological distress at exposure to internal or external cues that symbolize or resemble an aspect of the traumatic event.\n  - Physiological Reactions to Cues: Marked physiological reactions to internal or external cues that symbolize or resemble an aspect of the traumatic event.\n  - Internal Avoidance: Efforts to avoid distressing memories, thoughts, or feelings about or closely associated with the traumatic event.\n  - External Avoidance: Efforts to avoid external reminders (people, places, conversations, activities, objects, situations) that arouse distressing memories, thoughts, or feelings.\n▸ trauma_related_alterations\n  - Negative Self-Beliefs: Distorted beliefs such as 'I am fundamentally bad,' 'I am damaged,' or 'I am worthless'.\n  - Negative Beliefs about Others: Distorted beliefs such as 'No one can be trusted' or 'Others are dangerous'.\n  - Negative Worldview: Extreme beliefs such as 'The world is completely dangerous' or 'Life is meaningless'.\n  - Persistent Fear: A state of constant fear, anxiety, and tension.\n  - Persistent Horror: The shock and terror of the traumatic moment do not fade and remain.\n  - Persistent Anger: A state of chronic anger boiling inside or being expressed aggressively.\n  - Persistent Guilt: Unceasing feelings of guilt or remorse related to the trauma.\n  - Persistent Shame: Continuing to feel shame or humiliation due to the trauma.\n▸ arousal_and_reactivity\n  - Reduced Concentration: Unable to pay attention or maintain focus on specific activities like conversation, reading, or watching TV.\n  - Indecisiveness: Extreme difficulty making even minor daily choices (e.g., menu, clothes) and hesitating to make decisions.\n  - Subjective Memory Complaints: Complaining of memory decline, such as forgetting where things were placed, due to reduced focus.\n  - Clouded Thinking: Thinking feels foggy, dazed, or not clear.\n  - Impulsive Anger: Sudden explosions of anger for trivial reasons or no clear reason.\n  - Verbal Aggression: Attacking others with shouting, cursing, or blame without holding back.\n  - Physical Aggression: Physically attacking people or throwing/breaking objects.\n  - Chronic Irritability: A state where nerves are always sharp and sensitive, ready to explode at the slightest touch.\n  - Risky Behaviors: Reckless actions disregarding consequences, such as speeding, dangerous sports, or criminal acts.\n  - Substance Abuse: Excessive drinking or drug use to harm oneself.\n  - Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior: Engaging in unprotected or dangerous sexual activities repetitively.\n  - Self-Harm: Actions that intentionally harm or scar one's own body.\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - Duration: 3 months\n\nRules:\n- If the patient can specifically answer based on the profile, set matched=true, list related\n  sections/items in matched_sections, and write a short English strategy in answer_strategy\n  on what tone and content the patient should reply with.\n- If the question is not in the profile or can only be inferred, set matched=false and instruct\n  in answer_strategy to respond briefly (e.g., \"I'm not sure\", \"I haven't thought about it\").\n- If patient_turn_index is 1 (patient's first reply), ONLY focus the strategy on the chief complaint\n  (do NOT reveal other symptoms).\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"matched\": true|false, \"matched_sections\": [\"...\"], \"answer_strategy\": \"...\"}\n"

On top of that, I'm living in constant, exhausting fear and carrying a heavy burden of guilt and shame over what happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any persistent feelings of sadness, hopelessness, or loss of interest in activities you once enjoyed, beyond the feelings of guilt and shame related to the event?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel a general sense of sadness or a loss of interest in things on their own. Instead, I just feel completely consumed by the constant guilt, shame, and fear directly related to the trauma. Those feelings are so overwhelming that they don't leave room for anything else.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any difficulty sleeping, irritability, or difficulty concentrating, and do you find yourself constantly 'on guard' or easily startled, as if you're always waiting for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: I'm having a really hard time concentrating on daily tasks and conversations, and my nerves are so sharp that I feel constantly irritable. It's really just the lack of focus and this constant irritability that are making things so difficult.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any unusual perceptions, such as hearing voices or seeing things that others don't, or do you have any strong beliefs that seem unreal or illogical to others, especially in relation to the traumatic event?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that at all. I don't hear voices or see things that aren't there, and I don't hold any beliefs that are unreal or illogical. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.
